)
and
\|\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(i)}\|\leq 1
(from the definition of
\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(i)}
and the assumption of Lipschitz continuity
4.2
)
and are
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable; (from the definition of
\mathbf{d}_{k}
and
\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(i)}
)

\mathbf{d}_{k}\equiv\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\ast}-\mathbf{w}_{k}}{D},\quad\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(i)}\equiv\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}=1}^{\tau}\frac{\nabla f_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k,\tau^{\prime}}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k})}{2L}\mathds{1}_{k}+\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}=1}^{\tau}\frac{\nabla g_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k,\tau^{\prime}}^{(i)})-\nabla g_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k})}{2L}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

4. the conditional 1-subgaussian random variable
\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}

such that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]=0

and
\mathbb{E}[\exp(\|\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\|^{2})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]\leq 2

and therefore

\ln\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\langle\mathbf{e},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\rangle)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]\leq\ln\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\|\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\|)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2},\forall\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
and is
\mathcal{F}_{t+1}
-measurable. (from the definition of
\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}
,
independence of all
\bm{\zeta}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}
and
\bm{\xi}_{k}\equiv(\bm{\xi}_{k}^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}

and the assumption of sub-Gaussianity of stochastic gradients
4.4
,
and
Lemma

B.7
for subgaussianity of random variables,

Lemma

B.8
for subgaussianity of the average of subgaussian random vectors)

\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\equiv\frac{\nabla f_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k,\tau}^{(i)},\bm{\zeta}_{k,\tau}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k,\tau}^{(i)})}{2\sigma_{g}/\sqrt{B_{g}}}\mathds{1}_{k}+\frac{\nabla g_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k,\tau}^{(i)},\bm{\zeta}_{k,\tau}^{(i)})-\nabla g_{i}(\mathbf{w}_{k,\tau}^{(i)})}{2\sigma_{g}/\sqrt{B_{g}}}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

2.1: terms in the upperbound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}

By using the polarization identity
Lemma

B.2
and
the established lower bound of
\eta\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{u}_{k}\|^{2}
and
the lowerbound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}\langle\mathbf{u}_{k},\mathbf{w}_{k}-\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\rangle
in the previous step,
dropping the nonnegative term
\frac{\|\mathbf{w}_{K}-\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\|^{2}}{2\eta}
,
noting the assumption of finite diameter of the parameter space
\Theta

4.3

then
\|\mathbf{w}_{0}-\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\|\leq D
,
we rearrange the terms and obtain the following upperbound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}+\epsilon}{2}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}-\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]-|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon K}{2}+\frac{D^{2}}{2\eta}+\eta L^{2}K+2\gamma L^{2}(E-1)K

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k})\|_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k}+\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})\|_{\infty}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{4\eta\sigma^{2}_{g}}{B_{g}}\cdot\frac{1}{E^{2}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|^{2}+\frac{4\gamma L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{2D\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\left\langle\mathbf{d}_{k},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle+\frac{4\gamma L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\tau\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{k,\tau}^{(i)},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]\mathds{1}_{k}

By choosing the local step size
\gamma=\frac{\eta}{E}
,
and applying triangle inequality
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\leq\|\mathbf{x}\|+\|\mathbf{y}\|
, then:

\displaystyle\frac{4\gamma L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|\leq\frac{4\eta L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|+\frac{4\eta L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\|\mathbf{z}_{k,E-1}^{(i)}\|

Noting
\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]
and
2\sqrt{xy}\leq x+y
for any
x,y\geq 0
, then we have:

\displaystyle\frac{4\eta L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|=\sum_{k\in[K]}2\sqrt{\eta L^{2}\times\frac{4\eta\sigma^{2}_{g}}{B_{g}}\cdot\frac{1}{E^{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}2\sqrt{\eta L^{2}\times\frac{4\eta\sigma^{2}_{g}}{B_{g}}\cdot\frac{1}{E^{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|^{2}}\leq\eta L^{2}K+\frac{4\eta\sigma^{2}_{g}}{B_{g}}\cdot\frac{1}{E^{2}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|^{2}

Hence, we can upperbound
\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}
as:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}+\epsilon}{2}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}-\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]-|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]-\frac{\epsilon K}{2}+\frac{D^{2}}{2\eta}+4\eta L^{2}K\left(1-\frac{1}{2E}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k})\|_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k}+\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})\|_{\infty}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]+\frac{8\eta\sigma^{2}_{g}}{B_{g}}\cdot\frac{1}{E^{2}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{2D\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\left\langle\mathbf{d}_{k},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle+\frac{4\eta L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\tau\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{k,\tau}^{(i)},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{4\eta L\sigma_{g}}{\sqrt{B_{g}}}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\|\mathbf{z}_{k,E-1}^{(i)}\|+\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]\mathds{1}_{k}

2.2: terms in the upperbound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}G(\mathbf{w}_{k})\mathds{1}_{k}

Noting
G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\mathds{1}_{k}=G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\mathds{1}_{G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\mathds{1}_{k}
,
applying
Lemma

B.4
of softmax mean
m(\mathbf{x},\alpha)
with softmax hyperparameter
\alpha\geq\frac{2\ln m}{\epsilon^{\prime}}

then
G(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})=\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})]_{i}-m(\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}},\alpha)\leq\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{2}
,
using

G(\mathbf{w};\mathcal{I}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})=\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})]_{i}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})]_{i}\leq\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})]_{i}\leq\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}G(\mathbf{w}_{k})\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\Big[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})+[G(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]

\displaystyle\quad+[G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]+[G(\mathbf{w}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]\Big]\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}}{2}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}+\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k}+\sum_{k\in[K]}[G(\mathbf{w}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]\mathds{1}_{k}

2.3: upperbounds of

\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k}
and

\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k},[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

By applying the established
Lemmas

B.7
and
B.8

for subgaussianity of random variables and subgaussianity of the average of subgaussian random vectors
with
4.4
,
and
Lemma

B.10

of maximal inequality of subgaussian random variables
with
\mathbf{z}_{k}\leftarrow\frac{\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{2\sigma_{\zeta}/\sqrt{B_{\zeta}}},\frac{\mathbf{g}_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{g}_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{2\sigma_{\zeta}/\sqrt{B_{\zeta}}}
,
\mathds{1}_{k}\leftarrow\mathds{1}_{k},[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]
and
\delta\leftarrow\frac{\delta}{8}
,
the following upperbounds hold with probability at least
1-3\times\frac{\delta}{12}=1-\frac{\delta}{4}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k}\leq 2\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k}\leq 2\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{\infty}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]\leq 2\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

2.4: upper bound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|^{2}

For brevity,
with the correspondence
t:=\text{ind}(k,i,\tau)\equiv k\cdot nE+i\cdot E+\tau
,
we define such a filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}}
by

\mathcal{G}_{-1}=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
and

\mathcal{G}_{k}=\mathcal{F}_{nE\lceil\frac{t}{nE}\rceil}
,
and
\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}:=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{E}}\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}
.
Then from the definition and properties of
\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}
listed
at the beginning of the Step 2, we have:

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}]=\vec{0},\quad\ln[\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}]]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2},\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},

and
\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}
is
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}
-measurable.
Moreover,
\mathbf{r}_{k}
is
\mathcal{G}_{k}
-measurable from definitions of
\mathbf{r}_{k}
and
\mathcal{G}_{k}
.
We rewrite the summation as:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|^{2}=4E\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|^{2}=4E\sum_{k\in[K]}X_{k}

where
X_{k}:=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|^{2}
.
Then using Jensen’s inequality with
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}=1,[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\geq 0

and
\mathbf{r}_{k}
is
\mathcal{G}_{k}
-measurable,
and the tower property of conditional expectation, we have the following inequality
when
\lambda\in(0,1)
:

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda X_{k}/2)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}]\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|^{2}/2)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}]\leq\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|^{2}/2)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}]\leq(1-\lambda)^{-1/2}

since by introducing an independent standard Gaussian
z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
using
\mathbb{E}_{z}[\exp(\lambda z^{2}/2)]=(1-\lambda)^{-1/2}
for
\lambda\in(0,1)
:

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|^{2}/2)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}_{z}[\exp(\sqrt{\lambda}\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|z)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}]]=\mathbb{E}_{z}[\mathbb{E}[\exp(\sqrt{\lambda}z\|\mathbf{z}_{k}^{(i)}\|)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k},z]]\leq\mathbb{E}_{z}[\exp(\lambda z^{2}/2)]=(1-\lambda)^{-1/2}

Therefore, noting
X_{k}
is
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}
-measurable, for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have:

-K\frac{\lambda}{2}+\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{k\in[K]}X_{k}\right)\right]=-\lambda\frac{K}{2}+\sum_{k\in[K]}\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}X_{k}\right)\mid\mathcal{G}_{k}\right]\leq\frac{K}{2}\left[-\lambda-\ln(1-\lambda)\right]\leq\frac{K}{4}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(1-\lambda)}

By using the Chernoff bound, and introducing
\psi^{\ast}(u):=\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1)}u\lambda-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(1-\lambda)}
, we have:

\ln\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k\in[K]}X_{k}\geq K(1+u/2)\right)\leq\inf_{\lambda>0}-\frac{K}{4}(2+u)\lambda+\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{k\in[K]}X_{k}\right)\right]\leq-\frac{K}{4}\psi^{\ast}(u)

By the characterization of sub-gamma random variables, see also equation (2.5) in section 2.4 sub-gamma random variables, on page 29 of
Boucheron et al. 2013
.
we have
\psi^{\ast}(u)=(\sqrt{1+u}-1)^{2}
with its inverse function

\psi^{\ast-1}(v)=2\sqrt{v}+v
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k\in[K]}X_{k}\geq K(1+\psi^{\ast-1}(v)/2)\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k\in[K]}X_{k}\geq K(1+\sqrt{v}+v/2)\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{K}{4}v\right)

By selecting
v=\frac{4\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{K}

and noting
1+\sqrt{v}+v/2\leq\frac{3}{2}+v
, with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{8}
, we have:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\left\|\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\|^{2}=4E\sum_{k\in[K]}X_{k}\leq 4E\cdot K(1+\sqrt{v}+v/2)\leq 4E\cdot K\left(\frac{3}{2}+v\right)=2E\left(3K+8\ln\frac{8}{\delta}\right)

2.5: upper bound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\left\langle\mathbf{d}_{k},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle

With the correspondence
t:=\text{ind}(k,i,\tau)\equiv k\cdot nE+i\cdot E+\tau
,
we define such intermediate quantities,

T=K\cdot n\cdot E
,

\mathbf{v}_{t}:=[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\mathbf{d}_{k}
,
\mathbf{z}_{t}:=\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}
,
and
S_{t}:=\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[t]}\langle\mathbf{v}_{t^{\prime}},\mathbf{z}_{t^{\prime}}\rangle
,

V_{t}:=\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[t]}\|\mathbf{v}_{t^{\prime}}\|^{2}
.
Then, since
\mathbf{z}_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t+1}
-measurable
and
\mathbf{v}_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable, then
S_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable,

V_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable.

Then for
M_{t}:=\exp\left(\lambda S_{t}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}V_{t}\right)
,
which is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable,
we show it is a supermartingale
by using Theorem 4.2.4 on page 189 of
Durrett 2019
and showing
that the following inequality holds:

\mathbb{E}[M_{t+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]=M_{t}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\rangle-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\|\mathbf{v}_{t}\|^{2}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]\leq M_{t}

Therefore, we have
\mathbb{E}[M_{T}]\leq M_{0}=1
by the tower property of conditional expectation,
which implies:

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\rangle\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\sum_{t\in[T]}\|\mathbf{v}_{t}\|^{2}\right)\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}KE\right)

since we have the following fact using
\|\mathbf{d}_{k}\|\leq 1
and
\sum_{i\in[\mathcal{I}_{k}]}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}^{2}\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}=1
:

\sum_{t\in[T]}\|\mathbf{v}_{t}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}^{2}\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\|\mathbf{d}_{k}\|^{2}\leq KE

By using the Chernoff bound, then for any
u>0
, we have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\rangle\geq u\right)\leq\exp\left(\inf_{\lambda>0}-\lambda u+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}KE\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2KE}\right)

By selecting
u=\sqrt{2KE\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}
, with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{8}
, we have:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\left\langle\mathbf{d}_{k},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle=\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\rangle\leq\sqrt{2KE\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}

2.6: upper bound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\tau\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{k,\tau}^{(i)},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle

With the correspondence
t:=\text{ind}(k,i,\tau)\equiv k\cdot nE+i\cdot E+\tau
,
we define such intermediate quantities,

T=K\cdot n\cdot E
,

\mathbf{v}_{t}:=[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\tau\mathbf{e}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\mathds{1}_{\tau\in[E-1]}
,
\mathbf{z}_{t}:=\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}
,
then by the same procedure of showing supermartingale, we also have

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\rangle\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\sum_{t\in[T]}\|\mathbf{v}_{t}\|^{2}\right)\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\frac{K}{3}(E-1)(E-3/2)(E-2)\right)

since we have the following fact using
\|\mathbf{e}_{k}\|\leq 1,\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\tau^{2}=\frac{1}{3}(E-1)(E-3/2)(E-2)
and
\sum_{i\in[\mathcal{I}_{k}]}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}^{2}\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}=1
:

\sum_{t\in[T]}\|\mathbf{v}_{t}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}^{2}\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\tau^{2}\|\mathbf{e}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\|^{2}\leq\frac{K}{3}(E-1)(E-3/2)(E-2)

By the same procedure of taking Chernoff bound,
with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{8}
, we have:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}\tau\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{k,\tau}^{(i)},\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\right\rangle=\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\rangle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2K}{3}(E-1)(E-3/2)(E-2)\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}

2.7: upper bound of
\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\|\mathbf{z}_{k,E-1}^{(i)}\|

With the correspondence
t:=\text{ind}(k,i,\tau)\equiv k\cdot nE+i\cdot E+\tau
,
we define such intermediate quantities,

T=K\cdot n\cdot E
,

a_{t}:=[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\mathds{1}_{\tau=E-1}
,
z_{t}:=\|\mathbf{z}_{k,\tau}^{(i)}\|
,
then by the same procedure of showing supermartingale, we also have

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{t\in[T]}a_{t}z_{t}\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\sum_{t\in[T]}a_{t}^{2}\right)\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}K\right)

Since
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}^{2}\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}=1
, we have:

\sum_{t\in[T]}a_{t}^{2}=\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}^{2}\sum_{\tau\in[E]}\mathds{1}_{\tau=E-1}^{2}=\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}^{2}\leq K

By the same procedure of taking Chernoff bound,
with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{8}
, we have:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}[\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i}\|\mathbf{z}_{k,E-1}^{(i)}\|=\sum_{t\in[T]}a_{t}z_{t}\leq\sqrt{2K\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}

2.8: upper bounds of
\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]\mathds{1}_{k}
and
\sum_{k\in[K]}[G(\mathbf{w}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})]\mathds{1}_{k}

With the abuse of notation,
we redefine a filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

by
\mathcal{G}_{0}=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
and
\mathcal{G}_{k}=\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{k-1},\mathcal{I}_{k-1},\mathcal{B}_{k-1})
,
where a sequence of subsets
(\mathcal{I}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\text{Unif}(\mathcal{C}_{m}(\mathcal{I}))

with
\mathcal{C}_{m}(\mathcal{I})=\{A\subseteq\mathcal{I}\mid|A|=m\}
for some
0<m\leq n
and
\mathcal{I}\equiv\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
are independently and identically sampled from
\mathcal{C}_{m}(\mathcal{I})
, and are independent of
the collection of random sample batches
\mathcal{B}_{k}\equiv\left((\bm{\xi}_{k}^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}},(\bm{\zeta}_{k,\tau}^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k},\tau\in[E]}\right)
at any time
k
.
Since
\mathbf{w}_{k}
is
\mathcal{G}_{k}
-measurable,
\mathcal{I}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k}
are
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}
-measurable,
we show that
F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k}),G(\mathbf{w}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k}),\mathds{1}_{k}\equiv\mathds{1}_{G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}}
are
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}
-measurable,
and satisfy the following exponential tail bounds with
C:=\sigma/([-\ln(1-m/n)]n)
for any
t\geq 0

by using
Lemma

B.13
under
4.9
.

\mathbb{P}(F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\geq t\mid\mathcal{G}_{k})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t}{C}\right),\quad\mathbb{P}(G(\mathbf{w}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\geq t\mid\mathcal{G}_{k})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t}{C}\right)

Keeping
\mathcal{G}_{k},\mathds{1}_{k}
unchanged,
and substituting
Y_{k}\leftarrow F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k}),G(\mathbf{w}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})

and
\delta\leftarrow\frac{\delta}{8},C\leftarrow\sigma/([-\ln(1-m/n)]n)
in the remark for
Lemma

B.14
, we have:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\right]\mathds{1}_{k}\leq\frac{2\sigma}{[-\ln(1-\frac{m}{n})]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}+\frac{2\sigma}{[-\ln(1-\frac{m}{n})]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}\cdot K

\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G(\mathbf{w}_{k})-G(\mathbf{w}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\right]\mathds{1}_{k}\leq\frac{2\sigma}{[-\ln(1-\frac{m}{n})]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}+\frac{2\sigma}{[-\ln(1-\frac{m}{n})]n}\ln\frac{1}{2\kappa}\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}

where
\kappa:=\frac{|\mathcal{S}|}{K}\in[0,1]

is the constraint-satisfied ratio and with such a convention of
\ln\frac{1}{|\mathcal{S}|}\cdot\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}=0
when
\kappa=0
.

Step 3. Establish Final Bounds

3.1: rearranging terms in final bounds

By selecting
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon-4\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}-\frac{4\sigma}{\left[-\ln\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)\right]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}
,
and rearranging the terms with the established bounds in Step 2:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}-\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]-|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon K}{2}+\frac{D^{2}}{2\eta}+4\eta L^{2}K\left(1-\frac{1}{2E}\right)

\displaystyle+2\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}\cdot K+\frac{16\eta\sigma_{g}^{2}}{B_{g}E}\left(3+\frac{8\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{K}\right)\cdot K

\displaystyle+2D\sigma_{g}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{B_{g}KE}}\cdot K+4\eta L\sigma_{g}\sqrt{\frac{2E}{3B_{g}K}\left(1-\frac{1}{E}\right)\left(1-\frac{3}{2E}\right)\left(1-\frac{2}{E}\right)\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}\cdot K

\displaystyle+4\eta L\sigma_{g}\cdot\frac{1}{E}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{B_{g}K}}\cdot K+\frac{2\sigma}{\left[-\ln\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)\right]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}\cdot K

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}G(\mathbf{w}_{k})\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\epsilon+\frac{2\sigma}{\left[-\ln\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)\right]n}\ln\frac{1}{2\kappa}\right)\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}

with probability at least
1-\delta
with
step sizes
\eta,\gamma
such that
\gamma=\frac{\eta}{E}
, and
softmax hyperparameter
\alpha\geq\frac{2\ln m}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

3.2: selecting step sizes
\eta,\gamma
, tolerance
\epsilon
and softmax hyperparameter
\alpha

By balancing the terms
\frac{D^{2}}{2\eta},4\eta L^{2}K
, we selecting step sizes as:

\eta=\frac{D}{L\sqrt{8K}},\quad\gamma=\frac{D}{LE\sqrt{8K}}

By upper bounding
1-\frac{1}{2E}\leq 1,\sqrt{(1-\frac{1}{E})(1-\frac{3}{2E})(1-\frac{2}{E})}\leq 1-\frac{9/4}{E+2}
,
ans substituting the global step size
\eta
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}-\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]-|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon K}{2}+\frac{DL}{\sqrt{K/8}}\cdot K+2\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}\cdot K

\displaystyle+\frac{D\sigma_{g}^{2}}{LBE\sqrt{K/32}}\left(3+\frac{8\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{K}\right)\cdot K+2D\sigma_{g}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{BKE}}\left(1+\frac{E}{\sqrt{6K}}\left(1-\frac{9/4}{E+2}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2KE}}\right)\cdot K

\displaystyle+\frac{2\sigma}{\left[-\ln\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)\right]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}\cdot K

Noting that for
E=1
, we can show that the above bound still holds without
\frac{E}{\sqrt{6K}}(1-\frac{9/4}{E+2})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2KE}}
in the last term
since terms with
\sum_{\tau\in[E-1]}
are 0 when
E=1
. For
E\geq 2
,
we note that
\frac{E}{\sqrt{6K}}(1-\frac{9/4}{E+2})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2KE}}\leq\frac{E}{\sqrt{6K}}
is valid.
Combining these two cases, and introducing the “effective” gradient variance
\bar{\sigma}_{g}^{2}:=\frac{\sigma_{g}^{2}/B_{g}}{L^{2}E}
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})]\mathds{1}_{k}\leq\epsilon\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}-\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]-|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{K}{2}\Big[-\epsilon+\frac{DL}{\sqrt{K/32}}\left[1+2\bar{\sigma}_{g}^{2}\left(3+\frac{8\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{K}\right)+\bar{\sigma}_{g}\sqrt{\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}\left(1+\frac{E}{\sqrt{6K}}\right)\right]+4\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4\sigma}{\left[-\ln\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)\right]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}\Big]

By letting the sum of constant terms to be 0, noting

\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon-4\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}-\frac{4\sigma}{\left[-\ln\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)\right]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}
,
we obtain the following tolerance:

\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{DL}{\sqrt{K/32}}\left[1+2\bar{\sigma}_{g}^{2}\left(3+\frac{8\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{K}\right)+\bar{\sigma}_{g}\sqrt{\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}\left(1+\frac{E}{\sqrt{6K}}\right)\right]

\epsilon=\frac{DL}{\sqrt{K/32}}\left[1+2\bar{\sigma}_{g}^{2}\left(3+\frac{8\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}{K}\right)+\bar{\sigma}_{g}\sqrt{\ln\frac{8}{\delta}}\left(1+\frac{E}{\sqrt{6K}}\right)\right]+4\sigma_{\zeta}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\frac{24Km}{\delta}}{B_{\zeta}}}+\frac{4\sigma}{\left[-\ln\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)\right]n}\ln\frac{32}{\delta}

Then, we obtain the following inequality with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]+|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]+\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k}^{\ast})\right]\mathds{1}_{k}\leq\epsilon\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}

We show
\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}\neq 0
, otherwise, from the above inequlaity,
and using the definition of
\mathds{1}_{k}\equiv\mathds{1}_{G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}}
, we have

G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}>0,[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]=1,\mathds{1}_{k}=0
for all
k\in[K]
,
which leads to a contradiction as follows:

0<\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]+|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]+0\leq\epsilon\cdot 0=0

Noting that
0\leq[G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}][1-\mathds{1}_{k}]
,
we have the following inequality with
\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}\neq 0
:

\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k}^{\ast})\right]\mathds{1}_{k}\leq|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]+\sum_{k\in[K]}\left[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}_{k}^{\ast})\right]\mathds{1}_{k}\leq\epsilon\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}

By introducing a probability measure
\mathbf{P}_{K}
on the set of iterations
[K]
such that
\mathbf{P}_{K}(k)=\mathds{1}_{k}/\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k},\forall k\in[K]
,
and using the convexity of
F,G
(
4.1
and
Lemma

B.3
) and defining
\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{K}:=\mathbb{E}_{k\sim\mathbf{P}_{K}}[\mathbf{w}_{k}]=\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathbf{w}_{k}\mathds{1}_{k}/\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}
, then:

F(\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{K})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})\leq\mathbb{E}_{k\sim\mathbf{P}_{K}}\left[F(\mathbf{w}_{k})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})\right]\leq\epsilon

G(\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{K})\leq\mathbb{E}_{k\sim\mathbf{P}_{K}}[G(\mathbf{w}_{k})]\leq\epsilon+\frac{2\sigma}{[-\ln(1-\frac{m}{n})]n}\ln\frac{1}{2\kappa}

when the softmax hyperparameter
\alpha\geq\frac{2\ln m}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
is large enough.

3.3: analyzing the constraint-satisfied ratio
\kappa

Since
|\mathcal{S}|=\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}>0
has been shown,
we have the constraint-satisfied ratio
\kappa=\frac{|\mathcal{S}|}{K}\in(0,1]

and therefore
\kappa\geq\frac{1}{K}
in the analysis of the worst case.
By rearranging the terms, and noting that
\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathds{1}_{k}=\kappa\cdot K
and
\sum_{k\in[K]}[1-\mathds{1}_{k}]=(1-\kappa)\cdot K
:

|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|(1-\kappa)\leq\left(F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})-F(\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{K})+\epsilon\right)\kappa

Therefore, we can establish the upper bound of
\frac{1}{\kappa}
when
-G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})=|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|>0
:

\frac{1}{\kappa}\leq 1+\frac{F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})-F(\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{K})+\epsilon}{|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|}

Suppose that Slater’s condition holds, i.e., there exists
\mathbf{w}^{\ast}\in\Theta
and some
\nu>0

such that
G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})\leq-\nu<0
, then:

\ln\frac{1}{2\kappa}\leq\ln\min\left(\frac{K}{2},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{F(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})-F(\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{K})+\epsilon}{2|G(\mathbf{w}^{\ast})|}\right)\leq\ln\min\left(\frac{K}{2},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{DL+\epsilon}{2\nu}\right)

∎

Appendix F
Experimental Details

F.1  Neyman Pearson Classification

We consider the constrained minimax optimization problem fomulated in (

†

‣
2
), where the objective is to minimize the empirical loss on the majority class while ensuring the minority class loss remains below a prescribed tolerance. For each client
i
, the local objective and constraint are defined as

f_{i}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{m_{i,0}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{i}^{(0)}}\phi(\mathbf{w};(x,0)),\qquad g_{i}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{m_{i,1}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{i}^{(1)}}\phi(\mathbf{w};(x,1)),

where
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{(1)}
denote the local datasets for class-0 and class-1, respectively, and
m_{i,0}
and
m_{i,1}
are their corresponding cardinalities. The function
\phi
represents the binary logistic loss:

\phi(\mathbf{w};(x,y))=-y\,\mathbf{w}^{\top}x+\log\!\left(1+e^{\mathbf{w}^{\top}x}\right),\qquad y\in\{0,1\}.

This formulation captures the NP paradigm:
f(\textbf{w})
drives performance on the majority class, while the constraint
g(\mathbf{w})\leq\epsilon
ensures that the minority class loss does not exceed the predefined tolerance.

We evaluate our approach using the Breast Cancer dataset
[
Wolberg et al. 1993
]
, which contains 569 samples with 30 features. We allocate 80% of the data for training and reserve the remaining 20% for testing. The training data is distributed in an independent and identically distributed (IID) manner across
n=20
clients, ensuring each client receives an equal number of samples and an identical class distribution.

Algorithm performance is measured by tracking the majority-class objective loss
f(\mathbf{w})
, the minority-class constraint loss
g(\mathbf{w})
, and the number of rounds where the feasibility condition
g(\mathbf{w})\leq\epsilon
is violated. As illustrated in
Figure

1
, our algorithm successfully achieves convergence of the objective while satisfying the constraint.

. Global step sizes were selected from the set

.

With regards to the softmax parameter
\alpha
, the
For centralized training, all training data is aggregated locally and trained for
K
rounds, which is equivalent to the federated setting with a single local epoch (
E=1
) and full client participation.

In
Algorithm

1
, the switching criterion is introduced as
G_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{k};\mathcal{I}_{k})\leq\epsilon/2
. The
\epsilon/2
threshold was primarily selected for analytical convenience to strictly bound the estimation error along with the tolerance term. In practice, however, using a denominator of
2
was found to be overly conservative, hindering overall performance. Because any denominator greater than 1 preserves the theoretical scaling of the estimation error bounded by
\epsilon
, we relaxed the threshold to
\epsilon/1.1
.This empirical adjustment prevents overly pessimistic switching while ensuring robust constraint satisfaction.

F.2  Additional NP Classification Result

Figure 4
:
Federated learning settings.
Impact of the number of local epochs
E
(top row) and the client participation ratio
m/n
(bottom row).

As shown in
Figure

4
(top row), the algorithm demonstrates strong robustness to the number of local epochs
E
. This stability arises because the method effectively controls the scale of the update vector by averaging over the local steps. Moreover, higher client participation rates consistently accelerate convergence, while lower participation rates hinder strict constraint satisfaction. Because the softmax operation on a randomly sampled subset effectively evaluates an expectation, diluting the true worst-case value. This dilution biases the switching mechanism heavily toward objective minimization, which corroborates the behavior observed with smaller
\alpha
values in
Figure

2
.

Fair classification task is formulated by the local objective of binary cross entropy loss and constraint of demographic parity:

f_{i}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[y\log(\pi(x;\mathbf{w}))+(1-y)\log(1-\pi(x;\mathbf{w}))\right]

g_{i}(\mathbf{w}):=\left|\frac{1}{m_{i,p}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{i,p}}\pi(x;\mathbf{w})-\frac{1}{m_{i,u}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{i,u}}\pi(x;\mathbf{w})\right|,

where the subscript
p
and
u
represent protected and unprotected groups respectively. Since the demographic parity is defined as the absolute difference between the average logits of protected and unprotected groups, the aggregation at the server is treated with extra care. At the server, the average logits were aggregated instead of the final constraint value to recalculated the weighted average of logits for the global constraint calculation:

g(\mathbf{w}):=\left|\frac{1}{m_{\mathcal{S},p}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S},p}}\pi(x;\mathbf{w})-\frac{1}{m_{\mathcal{S},u}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S},u}}\pi(x;\mathbf{w})\right|,

where the subscript
\mathcal{S}
denote all sampled clients. Here, we explore the setting of stochastic data sampling and deep neural network, making the problem highly non-convex, stochastic, and non-smooth. The experiments were conducted on Adult dataset
[
Kohavi and Becker 1996
]
. For the penalty-based methods, the penalty parameter was chosen from
\rho\in[0.1,1.0,10.0,100.0]
and only the best results are presented. The detailed hyperparameters are found in
Table

2
.

Appendix G
Related Work

Federated Learning.

Since the introduction of decentralized learning by
McMahan et al. 2017
, Federated Learning (FL) has emerged as a cornerstone of privacy-preserving AI. Its impact spans high-stakes sectors, from healthcare diagnostics
[
Rieke et al. 2020
,
Peng et al. 2024
]
and financial modeling
[
Wen et al. 2023
,
Chatterjee et al. 2023
]
to battery management systems
[
Wang et al. 2024
,
Zhu et al. 2024
]
. While most FL research targets unconstrained objectives, real-world deployments must often satisfy fairness requirements, safety protocols, or resource budgets
[
Du et al. 2021
,
Huang et al. 2024
,
Yang et al. 2022
]
. This drives the development of constrained FL frameworks that integrate these requirements directly into the training loop to ensure reliable and compliant learning.

There have been many variants of FedAvg
[
McMahan et al. 2017
]
to tackle different aspects of FL, such as data heterogeneity
[
Karimireddy et al. 2020
,
Seo et al. 2024
,
Morafah et al. 2024
]
, system heterogeneity
[
Gong et al. 2022
,
Li et al. 2020a
,
Wu et al. 2024
]
, and fairness
[
Li et al. 2021a
,
Badar et al. 2024
]
. Beyond FedAvg, ADMM-based FL algorithms have been explored to tackle heterogeneity in FL
[
Acar et al. 2021
,
Gong et al. 2022
,
Zhou and Li 2023
]
. Despite this extensive body of research, the literature is heavily skewed toward unconstrained scenarios. This leaves a significant gap in our understanding of how to effectively integrate rigorous functional constraints into the federated training process.

A common way to handle this setting is to cast the problem as a saddle-point formulation and apply primal-dual optimization methods
[
Nemirovski 2004
,
Chambolle and Pock 2011
,
Bertsekas 2014
,
Hamedani and Aybat 2021
,
Zhang and Lan 2022
,
Hounie et al. 2023
,
Boob and Khalafi 2024
]
. Although these techniques are well-supported by theory, they often depend heavily on careful tuning of the dual variables and, in many cases, assume access to a known upper bound on the optimal dual multiplier. In addition, such methods frequently require projecting both the primal and dual iterates onto bounded domains, which are rarely known a priori in practical applications and can complicate their implementation.

An alternative primal-only line of work is the switching (sub-)gradient method (SGM), originally introduced by
Polyak 1967
, which has since been explored in a variety of optimization settings. Subsequent studies, such as
Titov et al. 2018
,
Stonyakin et al. 2019
,
Alkousa 2020
, have combined SGM with mirror descent to improve its flexibility and performance. Later,
Lan and Zhou 2020a
,
Lan and Zhou 2020b
extended SGM to stochastic settings and established iteration complexity guarantees under both convex and strongly convex assumptions. More recently, the theoretical analysis has been pushed further to cover weakly convex objectives, as shown in works like
Huang and Lin 2023
,
Liu and Xu 2025
,
Ma et al. 2020
,
Jia and Grimmer 2025
. In the federated learning literature,
Islamov et al. 2025a
,
Upadhyay et al. 2026
developed an important variant of SGM that accounts for communication compression.

Handling statistical heterogeneity across local clients is a foundational challenge in distributed optimization.
From a statistical perspective, this non-IID landscape closely resembles learning from complex mixture distributions.
While prior works have extensively studied the theoretical dynamics and non-asymptotic guarantees of iterative weighting algorithms
(such as Expectation-Maximization) in mixed linear regression
[
Luo and Hashemi 2024
,
Luo and Hashemi 2025
,
Luo and Hashemi 2026
]
,
our framework shifts the focus.
Rather than aiming for maximum likelihood estimation,
we utilize smoothing techniques
[
Beck and Teboulle 2012
]
such as
dynamic softmax weighting
[
Wang et al. 2023
]
to enforce worst-case distributionally robust optimization under strict operational constraints.

Minimax Optimization.

The mathematical foundations of minimax optimization were established by the seminal work of
Von Neumann 1928
, which proved the existence of saddle-point equilibria in zero-sum games. Early first-order approaches, such as the primal-dual methods of Arrow and Hurwicz, laid the groundwork for Gradient Descent-Ascent (GDA). However, GDA often fails to converge even in simple bilinear settings, a challenge that was later addressed by
Korpelevich 1976
through the introduction of the
Extragradient Method
, which remains the gold standard for smooth saddle-point problems.

In the era of large-scale machine learning, research has shifted toward stochastic regimes.
Nemirovski et al. 2009
and
Juditsky et al. 2011
established the optimal
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-2})
stochastic oracle complexity for convex-concave problems, which characterizes the fundamental limits of first-order stochastic optimization. Recently, this framework has been adapted to decentralized environments through
Agnostic Federated Learning

[
Mohri et al. 2019
]
, which employs a minimax objective to ensure model robustness across heterogeneous clients. Similarly,
Li et al. 2020b
proposed the
q
-FFL framework to achieve fair resource allocation using weighted optimization objectives.

Our formulation is closely related to
Group Distributionally Robust Optimization
(Group-DRO), where the objective is to minimize the loss under the worst-case distribution among a set of pre-defined groups. While traditional DRO solvers often rely on dual reformulations that can be sensitive to noise in federated settings, our switching mechanism provides a robust, primal-only alternative that maintains stability even under heavy client heterogeneity and partial participation.

To contextualize our work within the broader optimization literature, we observe that our problem formulation is fundamentally a specialized instance of
Group Distributionally Robust Optimization
(Group-DRO), similar to the frameworks discussed by
Sagawa et al. 2020
, where the uncertainty set is defined over the convex hull of discrete client distributions. Our objective is further aligned with the
Agnostic Federated Learning
(AFL) paradigm introduced by
Mohri et al. 2019
, which seeks to minimize the loss of the worst-performing client distribution to ensure global model robustness. However, while AFL and traditional DRO solvers often rely on primal-dual updates
[
Li et al. 2021b
,
Huang et al. 2025
]
that can become unstable under the “dual drift” inherent in partial participation, our approach adopts the logic-based
Switching Gradient
mechanism established by
Lan and Zhou 2020a
,
Lan and Zhou 2020b
.

The current state-of-the-art for handling expectation-constrained stochastic problems is the
Switching Gradient
mechanism developed by
Lan and Zhou 2020a
,
Lan and Zhou 2020b
. This approach avoids the inherent instability of dual-variable drift by alternating updates between the objective and the constraint based on a feasibility trigger. Our work represents the latest advancement in this lineage; by integrating a softmax-weighted smoothing technique, we extend the switching framework to the partial participation regime. Notably, we improve the high-probability complexity from the
\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}\frac{1}{\delta})
found in existing literature to a sharper
\mathcal{O}(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
guarantee, providing the most robust theoretical bound for constrained federated minimax problems to date.
